a context in which this result is true without these additional assumptions

7
Ascending Sequences of Finite Dimensional Banach (Partial) Poisson-Lie Groupoids and Examples

This section proposes an adaptation of some previous results to ascending sequences of partial Banach Poisson Lie groupoids with some examples.

7.1
Ascending Sequences of Banach (partial) Poisson-Lie Groupoids

As in
\lx@sectionsign

3.4.1
and for the same reasons, we only consider sequences of Banach partial Poisson Lie groupoids. Thus, naturally, an ascending sequence of Banach Poisson Lie groupoids
\{(T^{\flat}\mathcal{G}_{i},\mathcal{G}_{i},P_{i})\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is an ascending sequence of Banach (partial) Poisson manifolds (cf. Definition
3.28
) where

\bullet\;
each
\mathcal{G}_{i}\rightrightarrows M_{i}
is a finite dimensional Lie groupoid ;

\bullet\;

\left(M_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is an ascending sequence of manifolds;

\bullet\;
each
\mathcal{G}_{i}
is provided with source and target maps
{\bf s}_{i}
,
{\bf t}_{i}
, inverse
{\bf i}_{i}
, multiplications
{\bf m}_{i}
and unity maps
{\bf 1}_{i}
;

\bullet\;
for each
i
, any of these maps at level
i
is the restriction of the corresponding map at level
{i+1}
.

At first, recall the following results of ascending sequences of finite dimensional Lie groupoids:

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
\left(\mathcal{G}_{i}\rightrightarrows M_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a direct sequence of finite dimensional Banach-Lie groupoids. Then we have:

(1)

\mathcal{G}=\underrightarrow{\lim}\mathcal{G}_{i}
and
M=\underrightarrow{\lim}M_{i}
are Hausdorff convenient manifolds.

(2)

{\bf 1}=\underrightarrow{\lim}\mathbf{1}_{i}
is a smooth injective closed immersion from
M=\underrightarrow{\lim}M_{i}
into
\mathcal{G}
;

(3)

{\bf s}=\underrightarrow{\lim}\mathbf{s}_{i}
(resp.
{\bf t}=\underrightarrow{\lim}\mathbf{t}_{i}
) is a submersion from
\mathcal{G}
on to
M
;

(4)

{\bf m}=\underrightarrow{\lim}\mathbf{m}_{i}
is a smooth map from the convenient manifold
\mathcal{G}^{(2)}=\underrightarrow{\lim}\mathcal{G}^{(2)}_{i}
to
\mathcal{G}
;

(5)

{\bf i}=\underrightarrow{\lim}\mathbf{i}_{i}
is a smooth diffeomorphism of
\mathcal{G}
.

In fact, we obtain a natural structure of convenient groupoid
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
which is called the
direct limit Lie groupoid of the ascending sequence of

\left(\mathcal{G}_{i}\rightrightarrows M_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
. Its Lie algebroid is the direct limit of the sequence

\left(\mathcal{AG}_{i},M_{i},\rho_{i},[\;,\;]_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of Banach Lie algebroids.

Remark 7.2
.

Since
\mathcal{G}
(resp.
M
) is a direct limit of finite dimensional manifolds, it follows that the typical model of
\mathcal{G}
(resp.
M
) is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
which is a convenient reflexive vector space (cf.
[
KuMcK89
]
).

Theorem 7.3
.

Let
\left(\mathcal{G}_{i}\rightrightarrows M_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be an ascending sequence of finite dimensional Lie groupoids and
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
its direct limit Lie groupoid (cf. Proposition
7.1
).
Then, for each
x=\underleftarrow{\lim}x_{i}\in M
, we have:

(1)

The isotropy group
\mathcal{G}(x)
is a convenient Lie group which is the direct limit of the sequence of isotropy groups

\left(\mathcal{G}_{i}(x_{i})\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
.

(2)

The orbit
\mathcal{G}.x
is a closed immersed convenient submanifold of
M
which is the direct limit of the sequence of orbits

\left((\mathcal{G}_{i}).x_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
.

(3)

The restriction
{\bf t}_{x}:={\bf t}_{|\mathcal{G}(x,-)}:\mathcal{G}(x,-)\to M
is a
\mathcal{G}(x)
principal bundle which is the projective limit of the sequence of principal bundles
({\bf t}_{i})_{x_{i}}:\mathcal{G}_{i}(x_{i},-)\to M_{i}.

(4)

The associated convenient Lie algebroid
(\mathcal{AG},M,\rho,[\;,\;])
is split and, for any
x\in M
,
its orbit
\mathcal{G}.x
is a closed immersed submanifold of
M
whose connected components are orbits of the Banach-Lie algebroid
\mathcal{AG}
.

(5)

\mathcal{AG}^{\prime}=\underrightarrow{\lim}\mathcal{AG}_{i}^{*}
is the dual of
\mathcal{AG}
and
(\mathcal{AG},\mathcal{AG}^{\prime})
is a partial bialgebroid.

By application of Theorem
3.31
in the one hand, and application of
Theorem
7.3
to Theorem
2.24
, on the other hand, we obtain:

Corollary 7.4
.

Let
\left(\mathcal{G}_{i}\rightrightarrows M_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be an ascending sequence of finite dimensional Poisson (resp. symplectic) Lie groupoids and
\left(T^{\flat}\mathcal{G},T\mathcal{G},P\right)
be
the associated partial convenient direct limit manifold defined in Proposition
3.30
. Then in each case,
T^{\flat}\mathcal{G}=T^{\prime}\mathcal{G}
and in the symplectic case,
\mathcal{G}
is a (strong) symplectic
19
19

19

Since the symplectic form
\omega
on
\mathcal{G}
is the direct limit of
\omega_{i}
and as each
\omega_{i}^{\flat}
is an isomorphism so the direct limit of these operators is an isomorphism.
groupoid. Moreover all the Assertions of Theorm
4.7
(resp. Therorem
4.9
) are true and in particular the last one without anymore assumptions.

7.2
Examples

1.

Consider
(\mathcal{G}_{i}\rightrightarrows M_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be an ascending sequence of finite dimensional Poisson Lie groupoids. Then the associated sequence

\left(T^{*}\mathcal{G}_{i}\rightrightarrows\mathcal{AG}^{*}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of cotangent groupoids. It is also an ascending sequence of finite dimensional symplectic Lie groupoids. If
\mathcal{G}=\underrightarrow{\lim}\mathcal{G}_{i}
, then we have
T^{\prime}\mathcal{G}=\underrightarrow{\lim}T^{*}\mathcal{G}_{i}
and
\mathcal{AG}^{\prime}=\underrightarrow{\lim}\mathcal{AG}_{i}^{*}
and we have a structure of (strong) symplectic convenient Lie groupoid
T^{\prime}\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows\mathcal{AG}^{\prime}
.

2.

Let
\left(\mathsf{G}_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be an ascending sequence of finite dimensional Lie groups. Then the sequence of cotangent bundles
\left(T^{\prime}\mathsf{G}_{i}\right)_{i\in N}
is also an ascending sequence of finite dimensional bundles, each one having a structure of symplectic Lie groupoid (cf. Theorem
6.7
or
[
Marl05
]
, 1).
If
\mathsf{G}=\underrightarrow{\lim}\mathsf{G}_{i}
is also a convenient Lie group provided with an exponential map (cf. Theorem
3.33
), then
T^{\prime}\mathcal{G}=\underrightarrow{\lim}T^{*}\mathcal{G}_{i}
is the cotangent bundle of
\mathsf{G}
(cf.
Proposition
3.30
). It follows that
T^{\prime}\mathsf{G}
has also a structure of convenient Lie groupoid on
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\times\mathfrak{g}^{\flat}
(cf. Theorem
6.7
). Note that the adjoint action of
\mathsf{G}
on
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is the direct limit of the action of
\mathsf{G}_{i}
over
\mathfrak{g}_{i}^{\prime}
and so
\mathcal{O}_{\xi}
through
\xi=\underrightarrow{\lim}\xi_{i}
is
\underrightarrow{\lim}\mathcal{O}_{\xi}
and so is a split convenient submanifold of
\mathsf{G}
. It follows that all assertions of Theorem
6.7
are true, in particular, the last ones without any more assumptions.

For a more precise example, take
\mathcal{G}_{n}=\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{C})
. The following description refers to
[
Bab10
]
.

Using the left translation on
\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{C})
, the tangent space
T\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{C})
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{C})\times\mathfrak{gl}(n,\mathbb{C})

via
the right action of
\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{C})
. By the duality bracket
(A,B)\mapsto\mathsf{Tr}(AB)
, we can identify
\mathfrak{gl}^{*}(n,\mathbb{C})
with
\mathfrak{gl}(n,\mathbb{C})
and so the cotangent bundle of
\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{C})
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{GL}(\infty)\times\mathfrak{gl}(\infty)
.
Via
the duality bracket
(A,B)\mapsto\mathsf{Tr}(AB)
, we can identify
T^{\prime}\mathcal{GL}(n,\mathbb{C})
with
\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{C})\times\mathfrak{gl}(n,\mathbb{C})
. Therefore, the coadjoint orbit of
\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{C})
on
\mathfrak{gl}^{*}(n,\mathbb{C})
can be identified with the adjoint orbit of
\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{C})
on
\mathfrak{gl}(n,\mathbb{C})
.
Of course, the canonical symplectic form on
T^{*}(\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{C})\times\mathfrak{gl}(n,\mathbb{C}))
gies rise to a symplectic groupoid structure on
\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{C})\times\mathfrak{gl}(n,\mathbb{C})\rightrightarrows\mathfrak{gl}(n,\mathbb{C})\times\mathfrak{gl}(n,\mathbb{C})
. In particular, all the assertions of the theorem, even the last one, is true without any more assumptions.

On the one hand, each
A\in\mathfrak{gl}(n,\mathbb{C})
can be considered as a linear map on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
and let
J_{A}
be its normal Jordan form. Then we have
A=gJ_{A}g^{-1}
for some
g\in\mathsf{GL}(n,\mathbb{C})
. So the adjoint orbit
\mathcal{O}_{A}
of
A
is equal to
\mathcal{O}_{J_{A}}
. Thus any adjoint orbit is parametrized by all Jordan matrices.

On the one hand, the Kirilov-Kostant-Souriau symplectic form on
\mathcal{O}_{A}
is defined in the following way (cf.
[
Bab10
]
):

\sigma_{A}(X_{1},X_{2})=\mathsf{Tr}(\dot{g}_{1}g\dot{A}_{2})=-\mathsf{Tr}(\dot{g}_{2}g\dot{A}_{1})

(7.1)

where, for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
X_{i}
is a vector tangent to a curve on
[0,\varepsilon[
of type
A_{i}(t)=g_{i}(t)J_{A}g_{i}^{-1}(t)
given by:

g_{i}(0)=g,\;\;\dot{g}_{i}:=\dfrac{d}{dt}_{|t=0}g_{i}(t),\;\;\dot{A}_{i}:=\dfrac{d}{dt}_{|t=0}A_{i}(t).

It follows that the Lie algebra
\mathsf{GL}(\infty,\mathbb{C})=displaystyle\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathsf{GL}(n\mathbb{C})
is
\mathfrak{gl}(\infty,\mathbb{C})=\displaystyle\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{gl}(n\mathbb{C})
.
Therefore, for
A
and
B
in
\mathfrak{gl}(\infty,\mathbb{C})
, the product is well defined since we have an integer
n
such that
A
and
B
belongs to the same
\mathfrak{gl}(n,\mathbb{C})
(
via
the successive inclusions
\iota_{n}
of
\mathfrak{gl}(n\mathbb{C})
into
\mathfrak{gl}(n\mathbb{C})
and so the bilinear form

\mathsf{Tr}(AB):=\mathsf{Tr}_{n}(AB)

is well defined if
\mathsf{Tr}_{n}
is the bilinear operator trace in
\mathfrak{gl}(n\mathbb{C})
.
This gives rise to an operator of duality between
\mathfrak{gl}(\infty,\mathbb{C})
and
\mathfrak{gl}^{*}(\infty,\mathbb{C})
which is compatible with the previous inclusion
\iota_{n}
and so is compatible with the direct limit. For analogue reasons, the adjoint actions are compatible with the inclusions of
\mathsf{GL}(n\mathbb{C})
into
\mathsf{GL}(n\mathbb{C})
and
\iota_{n}
and so any coadjoint orbit can be identified with an adjoint orbit which implies that the same result is true for the direct limits. Thus the coadjoint orbits are parametrized by the union of all Jordan matrices of any finite dimension. Since each such orbit is contained in some
\mathfrak{gl}(n,\mathbb{C})
, the Kirillov-Kostant-Sauriau symplectic form is also given by the formulae (
7.1
).

Appendix A
Bounded Operators on a Hilbert Separable Complex Space and Von Neuman Algebras

Von Neumann algebras were originally introduced by John von Neumann. For a complete exposition on Von Neumann algebras, see Dixmier’s book
[
Dix69
]
.

A.1
Basic preliminaries

The following results on the space of bounded operators on a complex separable Hilbert space are classical but, for the sake of completeness, we refer to the concise and complete notes
[
Hou13
]
or also
[
Dix69
]
.

A.1.1
Basic Properties of Bounded Operators on a Separable Complex Hilbert Space

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable complex Hilbert space. We denote by
\langle\;|\;\rangle
its inner product and
||\;||
its associated norm. Let
\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
be the
Lie algebra Banach space of bounded linear operators
on
{\mathcal{H}}
provided with the classical norm
||A||_{\infty}=\sup_{||x||\leq 1}
and the Lie bracket of commutateur

[A,B]=AB-BA
where
AB
means the composition
A\circ B
.

We will enumerate some classical properties of bounded operators.

The adjoint of
A\in\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
is denoted
A^{*}
;

A\in\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
is called
self-adjoint
if
A=A^{*}
;

p\in\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
is called
a projection
if
p^{2}=p=p^{*}
;

A\in\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
is called
positive
if
\langle Ax|x\rangle\geq 0
for all
x\in{\mathcal{H}}
; in this case,
A
is self-adjoint;

for
A,B\in\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
we say that
A\geq B
if
A-B
is positive;

u\in\mathsf{L}({\mathcal{H}})
is an
isometry
if
uu^{*}=\operatorname{Id}
;

u\in\mathsf{L}({\mathcal{H}})
is
unitary
if
uu^{*}=u^{*}u=\operatorname{Id}
;

u\in\mathsf{L}({\mathcal{H}})
is a
partial isometry
if
u^{*}u
is a projection.

Lemma A.1
.

If
u
is a partial isometry then so is
u^{*}
. The subspace
u^{*}{\mathcal{H}}
is then closed and called the
initial domain
of
u
, the subspace
u{\mathcal{H}}
is also closed and called the
final domain
of
u
.

If
A
is positive then
A=A^{*}
; it has a square root
B:=A^{\frac{1}{2}}
i.e. an operator
B
such that
B^{2}=A
.

For any
A\in\mathsf{L}({\mathcal{H}})
, we set
|A|=(A^{*}A)^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

Lemma A.2
.

For any
A\in\mathsf{L}({\mathcal{H}})
, there exists a partial isometry
u
such that
A=u|A|
, and that
u
is unique subject to the condition that its initial domain is
(\ker A)^{\perp}
. The final domain of this
u
is
A{\mathcal{H}}=(\ker A^{*})^{\perp}
. Such a decomposition is called
the polar decomposition of
A
.

A.1.2
Some Classical Banach Sets of Bounded operators

The
trace
of a positive operator is

\operatorname{Tr}A:=\displaystyle\sum_{n}^{\infty}\langle e_{n}|Ae_{n}\rangle

where
\left(e_{n}\right)
is an orthonormal basis of
{\mathcal{H}}
. This value does not depend on the choice of the orthonormal basis.

The set
\mathsf{L}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})
of
Hilbert-Schmidt operators
is the subspace of
\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
of operators
A
such that
||A||_{2}:=(\operatorname{Tr}(AA^{*}))^{\frac{1}{2}}<\infty
. It is a Banach Lie algebra with the norm
||\;||_{2}
and the Lie bracket of commutator. It is an ideal of
\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
.

For any
A\in\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
, the square root of
(AA^{*})
is a well defined bounded operator and so the set
\mathsf{L}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
of
trace-class operators
is the subspace of operators
A
in
\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
such that

||A||_{1}:=\operatorname{Tr}(AA^{*})^{\frac{1}{2}}<\infty

It is a Lie algebra for the norm
||\;||_{1}
and with the Lie bracket of commutator
Note that we have
|\operatorname{Tr}A|\leq||A||_{1}
and so
\mathsf{L}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})\hookrightarrow\mathsf{L}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
.

For any
1<p<\infty
, let
\mathsf{L}_{p}({\mathcal{H}})
the subspace of
A\in\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
such that

||A||_{p}:=\left(\operatorname{Tr}(AA^{*})^{\frac{p}{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

It is a Lie algebra for the norm
||\;||_{p}
and the Lie bracket of commutator.

We have the following classical properties:

\bullet\;
for
1<p<2<q<\infty
, we have

\mathsf{L}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})\hookrightarrow\mathsf{L}_{p}({\mathcal{H}})\hookrightarrow\mathsf{L}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})\hookrightarrow\mathsf{L}_{q}({\mathcal{H}})\hookrightarrow\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}).

(A.1)

Note that
\mathsf{L}_{p}({\mathcal{H}})
is dense in
\mathsf{L}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})
and a closed subspace of
\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
but is not supplemented.

\bullet\;
The bilinear map
(A,B)\mapsto\operatorname{Tr}(AB)
defines

a strong duality
20
20

20

A non degenerate bounded bilinear
(x,y)\mapsto<x,y>
between convenient spaces
\mathbb{E}
and
\mathbb{F}
is called a
weak pairing
. Such a paring is called
strong
if the map
x\mapsto<x,\;>
(resp.
y\mapsto<\;,y>
) is a convenient isomorphism from
\mathbb{E}
(resp.
\mathbb{F}
) to the dual of
\mathbb{F}
(resp. of
\mathbb{E}
).
pairing between
\mathsf{L}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})
with itself;

a weak duality pairing between
\mathsf{L}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
and
\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
and, by the way,
\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
can be identified with the dual of
\mathsf{L}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
;

a strong duality pairing between
\mathsf{L}_{p}({\mathcal{H}})
and
\mathsf{L}_{q}({\mathcal{H}})
if
\dfrac{1}{p}+\dfrac{1}{q}=1
, and so the dual of
\mathsf{L}_{p}({\mathcal{H}})
can be identified with
\mathsf{L}_{q}({\mathcal{H}})
and conversely.

A.2
Finite Rank and Compact Operators

In this paragraph, the orthonormal basis
\left(e_{n}\right)
for
{\mathcal{H}}
is fixed. Let
\mathsf{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({\mathcal{H}})
be the set of operators
A\in\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
whose range is finite dimensional. On the other hand,
A\in\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
is called
compact
if the image of the closure of the unit ball in
{\mathcal{H}}
is relatively compact. We denote by
\mathsf{L}_{K}({\mathcal{H}})
the set of compact operators on
{\mathcal{H}}
. The following results on the space
\mathsf{L}_{K}({\mathcal{H}})
are classical but for the sake of completeness, we refer to the concise and complete notes of Bourbaki.

\bullet\;

\mathsf{L}_{K}({\mathcal{H}})
is the closure of
\mathsf{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({\mathcal{H}})
in
\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
relative to the norm
||\;\||_{\infty}
. In particular,
\mathsf{L}_{K}({\mathcal{H}})
is closed in
\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
.

\bullet\;

\mathsf{L}_{K}({\mathcal{H}})
is an ideal in the algebra
\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
. In particular,
\mathsf{L}_{K}({\mathcal{H}})
is a Lie subalgebra of
\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
.

\bullet\;

\mathsf{L}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})\subset\mathsf{L}_{K}({\mathcal{H}})
.

\bullet\;

\mathsf{L}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})=\left(\mathsf{L}_{K}({\mathcal{H}})\right)^{\prime}
and
\left(\mathsf{L}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})\right)^{\prime}=\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
.

Remark A.3
.

Consider the map
\Phi:\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{N})\to\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
given by:

\Phi(\lambda_{n})(x)=\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{n}\langle x|e_{n}\rangle e_{n}.

Then we have:

\Phi^{-1}(\mathsf{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({\mathcal{H}}))=c_{00}(\mathbb{N})
, that is the set of finite complex sequences;

\Phi^{-1}(\mathsf{L}_{K}({\mathcal{H}}))=c_{0}(\mathbb{N})
, that is the set of complex sequences which converge to
0
.

Moreover in each previous situation
\Phi
is an isometry.

It is well known that, for
1<p<2<q<\infty
, we have:

\ell^{1}(\mathbb{N})\hookrightarrow\ell^{p}(\mathbb{N})\hookrightarrow\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})\hookrightarrow\ell^{q}(\mathbb{N})\hookrightarrow c_{0}(\mathbb{N})\hookrightarrow\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{N})

where each inclusion is continuous.
On the other hand,
c_{00}(\mathbb{N})\subset\ell^{1}(\mathbb{N})
is a convenient space as a direct limit of ascending sequence of finite dimensional Banach spaces, say
\ell(n)
of finite sequences defined on
\{1,\dots,n\}
(cf. proof of Proposition
A.4
). Moreover,
c_{00}(\mathbb{N})
is dense in
\ell^{1}(\mathbb{N})
and its inclusion is continuous according to its convenient topology.

As in Remark
A.3
, we have a comparable situation for the previous spaces of bounded operators:

Proposition A.4
.

The set
\mathsf{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({\mathcal{H}})
has a structure of convenient space which is dense in
\mathsf{L}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
and the inclusion is continuous according to the convenient topology on
\mathsf{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({\mathcal{H}})
and the Banach structure on
\mathsf{L}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
. In particular, for
1<p<2<q<\infty
, we have the chain of continuous inclusions:

\mathsf{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({\mathcal{H}})\hookrightarrow\mathsf{L}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})\hookrightarrow\mathsf{L}_{p}({\mathcal{H}})\hookrightarrow\mathsf{L}_{2}({\mathcal{H}})\hookrightarrow\mathsf{L}_{q}({\mathcal{H}})\hookrightarrow\mathsf{L}_{K}({\mathcal{H}})\hookrightarrow\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}),

(A.2)

for
1<p<2<q<\infty
,
and
\Phi^{-1}(\mathsf{L}_{k}({\mathcal{H}}))=\ell^{k}(\mathbb{N})
, for any
1\leq k\leq\infty

Proof.
Let
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
be the vector subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}
generated by
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}
. We consider the canonical inclusion
\iota_{n}:{\mathcal{H}}_{n}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{H}}_{n+1}
. If
\mathsf{L}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})
is the Banach space of continuous endomorphisms of
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
, we have an evident inclusion
\iota_{n}^{L}:\mathsf{L}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})\hookrightarrow\mathsf{L}({\mathcal{H}}_{n+1})
. By the way, the sequence
\{\mathsf{L}({\mathcal{H}}_{n}),\iota_{n}^{L}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is an ascending sequence of Banach spaces and so its direct limit
\underrightarrow{\lim}\mathsf{L}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})=\displaystyle\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathsf{L}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})
is contained in a convenient subspace of
\mathsf{L}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
. Since
\mathsf{L}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})
is a Banach subspace, each inclusion
\iota_{n}^{L}
of
\mathsf{L}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})
into
\mathsf{L}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
is continuous and so is the inclusion
\underrightarrow{\lim}\iota_{n}^{L}
of
\underrightarrow{\lim}\mathsf{L}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})
in
\mathsf{L}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
(cf.
[
CaPe23
]
, 5.4).

Note that
\underrightarrow{\lim}\mathsf{L}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathsf{L}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})
is also contained in
\mathsf{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({\mathcal{H}})
. Consider
A\in\mathsf{L}_{\operatorname{fin}}({\mathcal{H}})
. Then the Kernel
K
of
A
is finite codimensional and its range
R=A(E)
is finite dimensional. Therefore, thee exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
K+{\mathcal{H}}_{n}={\mathcal{H}}
and
R\subset{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
. Thus the
restriction
A_{n}
of
A
to
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
belongs to
\mathsf{L}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})
. Now, for any
m\geq n
, the restriction
A_{m}
to
{\mathcal{H}}_{m}
also belongs to
\mathsf{L}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})
and the restriction of

A_{m}
to
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
is
A_{n}
. It follows easily that
A=\underrightarrow{\lim}A_{n}
and, finally, we have

\underrightarrow{\lim}\mathsf{L}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})=\mathsf{L}_{fin}({\mathcal{H}})
,
which ends the proof of the first part.

Relation (
A.2
) is then a direct consequence of the previous result and (
A.1
). Finally, the last part is a consequence of Remark
A.3
and the definition of each Banach space
\mathsf{L}_{k}({\mathcal{H}})
for
1\leq k\leq\infty
.

A.3
Von Neuman Algebra and Groupoid

A.3.1
Definition of a Von Neumann Algebra

We begin by some recalls on some usual topologies on
\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
.

(1)

The
norm topology
defined norm
||\;||_{\infty}
.

(2)

The
strong operator topology
. A basis of neighbourhoods of
A\in\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
for this topology is

V(A,x_{1}\dots,x_{n},\varepsilon):=\{B\;:\;||B-Ax_{i}||_{\infty}<\varepsilon,\;\;\forall i=1,\dots,n\}.

(3)

The
weak operator topology
. A basis of neighbourhood of
A\in\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
for this topology is

V(A,x_{1}\dots,x_{n},\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{n},\varepsilon):=\{B\;:\;|\langle B-Ax_{i}|\eta_{i}\rangle|<\varepsilon,\;\;\forall i=1,\dots,n\}.

(4)

The
weak topology
is the topology of pointwise convergence on
{\mathcal{H}}
in the ”weak topology” on
{\mathcal{H}}
according to the inner product.

We have the following order between these topologies

\{\textrm{weak operator toplology}\}\;<\;\{\textrm{strong operator toplology}\}\;<\;\{\textrm{norm toplology}\}.

Definition A.5
.

A *-subalgebra unital
\mathfrak{M}

21
21

21

This means that
\mathfrak{M}
is stable by adjoint operation and contains the identity.
which is closed for the topology of weak operator topology of
\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
is called a
Von Neuman algebra
.

Remark A.6
.

For any Banach space
\mathbb{E}
, a predual of
\mathbb{E}
is a Banach space
\mathbb{E}_{*}
such that
(\mathbb{E}_{*})^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{E}
. In general, when a predual exists, it is not unique. We say that
\mathbb{E}_{*}
is a
strong unique predual
if
\mathbb{E}_{*}\subset\mathbb{E}^{\prime}
. S. Sakai (cf.
[
Sak71
]
) has shown that there exists an equivalence between weak closed *-subalgebras
\mathfrak{M}
of
\mathsf{L}({\mathcal{H}})
and
C^{*}
-algebras
\mathfrak{M}
of
\mathsf{L}({\mathcal{H}})
which having a strong unique predual
\mathfrak{M}_{*}
. For instance,
\mathsf{L}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
(resp.
(\mathsf{L}_{K}({\mathcal{H}})
) is the predual of
\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
(resp.
\mathsf{L}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
).

Examples A.7
.

1.

Any finite dimensional *-subalgebra of
\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
which contains
Id
.

2.

\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
.

3.

The algebra of essentially bounded measurable function on
([0,1],dx)

A.4
Groupoid Associated to a Von Neumann Algebra

For this paragraph, we refer to
[
OdSl16
]
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a von Neumann
algebra. We denote by
\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})
the set of projections of
\mathfrak{M}
and by
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{M})
the set of partial isometries of
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{M})
, that is,

\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M}):=\{p\in\mathfrak{M},\;:\;p=p^{2}=p^{*}\}\textrm{~~and~~}\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{M}):=\{u\in\mathfrak{M},\;:\;u^{*}u=1=uu^{*}\}.

\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})
is an ordered set by

p\leq q\Leftrightarrow pq=p.

In fact
\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})
is a lattice

22
22

22

A partially ordered set

{\displaystyle(L,\leq)}
is called a
lattice
if each two-element subset

{\displaystyle\{a,b\}\subseteq L}
has a least upper bound, denoted by

{\displaystyle a\vee b}
and greatest lower bound, denoted by

{\displaystyle a\wedge b}
.
.

For
x\in\mathfrak{M}
, we consider its polar decomposition
x=u|x|
. In fact, since
|x|=(x^{*}x)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, then
|x|
belongs to
\mathfrak{M}^{+}:=\{x\in\mathfrak{M},\;:\;x^{*}=x>0\}
and
u\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{M})
and so
{\bf s}(|x|):=u^{*}u
belongs to
\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})
. Note that
{\bf t}(|x^{*}|)=uu^{*}
also belongs to
\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})
.

We denote by
G(p\mathfrak{M}p)
the group of invertible elements of the Von Neuman subalgebra
p\mathfrak{M}p
of
\mathfrak{M}
and
U(p\mathfrak{M}p)
the group of invertible elements of
p\mathfrak{M}p
. Note that for
p=\operatorname{Id}
one has
p\mathfrak{M}p=\mathfrak{M}
, so the corresponding groups are simply denoted
G(\mathfrak{M})
and
U(\mathfrak{M})
respectively.

Note that for any
x\in\mathfrak{M}
,
|x|
belongs to
p\mathfrak{M}
where
p={\bf s}(|x|):=u^{*}u
. By the way, we consider the set

\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{M}):=\{x\in\mathfrak{M},\;:\;|x|\in\mathfrak{M}p,\;\;p={\bf s}(|x|)\}.

The following theorem summarizes some essential results proved in
[
OJS18
]
, 2:

Theorem A.8
.

1.

\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{M})
has a canonical structure of groupoid over
\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})
where, according to polar decompositions, we have:

(i)

the identity section
{\bf 1}:\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})\to\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{M})
is the inclusion;

(ii)

the source
{\bf s}
and the target
{\bf t}
from
\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{M})
to
\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})
are given by

{\bf s}(x)=u^{*}u
and
{\bf t}(x)=uu^{*}
;

(iii)

the multiplication
{\bf m}(x,y)
is the multiplication in the algebra
\mathfrak{M}
if
{\bf s}(x)={\bf t}(y)
;

[iv)

the inverse
{\bf i}:\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{M})\to\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{M})
is defined by

{\bf i}(x)=|x|^{-1}u^{*}
if the polar decomposition of
x
is
u|x|
.

2.

The set of partial isometries
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{M})
is a wide subgroupoid
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{M})\rightrightarrows\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})
.

A.5
Orbits of Von Neumann Algebra Groupoids

For this paragraph, we again refer to
[
OJS18
]
.

Given a Von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}
, recall that two projections
p,p^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{M}
are called
Murray-von Neumann equivalent
and denoted
p\sim p^{\prime}
if there exists
u\in\mathfrak{M}
such that
p=uu^{*}
and
p^{\prime}=u^{*}u
. Fix some
p_{0}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})
and set

\mathcal{L}_{p_{0}}:=\{p\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})\;:\;p\sim p_{0}\}\textrm{ and }\mathcal{G}_{p_{0}}(\mathfrak{M}):={\bf t}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{L}_{p_{0}}(\mathfrak{M}\right)\cap{\bf s}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{L}_{p_{0}}(\mathfrak{M})\right)

Then
\mathcal{G}_{p_{0}}(\mathfrak{M})\rightrightarrows\mathcal{L}_{p_{0}}(\mathfrak{M})
is a subgroupoid of
\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{M}\rightrightarrows\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})
. If
p\sim p_{0}
, then we have

\mathcal{G}_{p}(\mathfrak{M})\rightrightarrows\mathcal{L}_{p}(\mathfrak{M})
and
\mathcal{G}_{p_{0}}(\mathfrak{M})\rightrightarrows\mathcal{L}_{p_{0}}(\mathfrak{M})
coincide. From Theorem
4.4
and
[
OJS18
]
on the other hand, we have the following illustration of Theorem
4.4
in this context:

Theorem A.9
.

1.

Each
\mathcal{G}_{p_{0}}(\mathfrak{M})\rightrightarrows\mathcal{L}_{p_{0}}(\mathfrak{M})
is open and closed in the groupoid
\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{M})\rightrightarrows\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})
.

2.

The Banach Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{M})\rightrightarrows\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})
is a disjoint union of Banach Lie groupoids
\mathcal{G}_{p_{0}}(\mathfrak{M})\rightrightarrows\mathcal{L}_{p_{0}}(\mathfrak{M})
,
p_{0}\in\mathcal{L}\mathfrak{M})
. In particular, each set
\mathcal{L}_{p_{0}}(\mathfrak{M})
is an orbit of the groupoid
\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{M})\rightrightarrows\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})
.

3.

If
P_{0}=({\bf s}_{|\mathcal{G}_{p_{0}}(\mathfrak{M})})^{-1}(p_{0})
then
P_{0}\to\mathcal{L}_{p_{0}}(\mathfrak{M})
is a principal bundle with Lie group
G_{0}={\bf t}_{|P_{0}}^{-1}(p_{0})
.

To be more clear with the link between proofs in
[
OJS18
]
and direct applications of Theorem
4.4
, we end this section with the interpretations of these results in terms of the Von Neumann algebra context according to
[
OJS18
]
results in section 1.

For fixed
p_{0}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})
,
P_{0}
is an open set of
\mathfrak{M}p_{0}
.
G_{0}
is the group
G(p_{0}\mathfrak{M}p_{0})
of invertible elements of the Von Neumann subalgebra
p_{0}\mathfrak{M}p_{0}
.
We have a natural free right action of
G_{0}
on
p_{0}\mathfrak{M}p_{0}
which gives rise to a
G_{0}
principal bundle
P_{0}\to P_{0}/G_{0}
and
(P_{0}\times P_{0})\to(P_{0}\times P_{0})/G_{0}
which are isomorphic to
P_{0}\to\mathcal{L}_{p_{0}}(\mathfrak{M})
. In particular,
\mathcal{L}_{p_{0}}(\mathfrak{M})
(resp.
\mathcal{G}_{p_{0}}(\mathfrak{M})
) is isomorphic to
P_{0}/G_{0}
(resp.
(P_{0}\times P_{0})/G_{0}
). In fact, we have the following diagram of groupoid isomorphisms (
[
OJS18
]
, Proposition 1.4)

\textstyle{(P_{0}\times P_{0})/G_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}_{p_{0}}(\mathfrak{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{0}/G_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{p_{0}}(\mathfrak{M})}

A.6
Lie Poisson Structures on Von Neumann Algebra Groupoids

A von Neumann algebra also called a
W^{*}
-algebra is a
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{M}
which has a Banach predual space
\mathfrak{M}_{*}
, i.e.
\mathfrak{M}=(\mathfrak{M}_{*})^{*}
. Denote by
\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})
the lattice of projections
p
on
\mathfrak{M}
such that
p=p^{*}=p^{2}
. If
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{M})
is the set of all partial isometries

u
in
\mathfrak{M}
(i.e.
u^{*}u\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})
) for any
x\in\mathfrak{M}
such that
x=x^{*}
let
s(x)
be the smallest projection in
\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})
such that
s(x)x=x
. Let
\mathfrak{M}^{+}:=\{x\in\mathfrak{M}\;:\;x^{*}x>0\}
; we then have a polar decomposition
x=u|x|
where
u
belongs to
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{M})
and
|x|\in\mathfrak{M}^{+}
. One has
s(|x^{*}|)=uu^{*}
and
s(|x|)=u^{*}u
. In fact, for any
x\in\mathfrak{M}
, we have
|x|\in p\mathfrak{M}p
, where
p=s(|x|)
.

We consider the set
\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{M})=\{x\in\mathfrak{M}:|x|\textrm{ invertible in }p\mathfrak{M}p\}
. From the previous notations, we have well defined maps
\mathbf{s}:\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{M})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})
and
\mathbf{t}:\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{M})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})
respctively given by

\mathbf{s}(x)=u^{*}u
and
\mathbf{t}(x)=uu^{*}
. By the way we have a groupoid structure
\mathcal{G}\mathfrak{M}{\rightrightarrows}\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})
(for such a
complete description and results see
[
OdSl16
]
).

For fixed
p_{0}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{M})
we set
P_{0}=\mathbf{s}^{-1}(p_{0})
. Then
P_{0}
is an open set of
\mathfrak{M}p_{0}
. Thus the tangent bundle
TP_{0}
is isomorphic to
P_{0}\times\mathfrak{M}p_{0}
and its cotangent bundle
T^{*}P_{0}
is isomorphic to
P_{0}\times p_{0}\mathfrak{M}^{*}
.
Consider the predual
(\mathfrak{M}p_{0})_{*}=p_{0}\mathfrak{M}_{*}
of
(\mathfrak{M}p_{0})^{*}=p_{0}\mathfrak{M}^{*}
and so the bundle
P_{0}\times p_{0}\mathfrak{M}_{*}
is a weak Banach subbundle of

P_{0}\times p_{0}\mathfrak{M}^{*}
which defines a weak Banach subbundle
T_{*}P_{0}
of
T^{*}P_{0}
. We denote by
\iota:T_{*}P_{0}\to T^{*}P_{0}
the inclusion morphism.

Now according to
[
OJS18
]
, Proposition 5.2 and Remark 5.1, if
\omega
denotes the canonical symplectic form on
T^{*}P_{0}
then
\omega_{0}=\iota^{*}\omega
is a weak symplectic form on
T_{*}P_{0}
. More precisely, if we identify
T_{*}P_{0}
with
P_{0}\times p_{0}\mathfrak{M}_{*}
and
T^{*}P_{0}
with
P_{0}\times p_{0}\mathfrak{M}^{*}
, then
T(T_{*}P_{0})\equiv(P_{0}\times p_{0}\mathfrak{M}_{*})\times(\mathfrak{M}p_{0}\times p_{0}\mathfrak{M}_{*})
and the range of
\omega^{\flat}_{0}
is then

T^{\flat}(T_{*}P_{0})\equiv(P_{0}\times p_{0}\mathfrak{M}_{*})\times(\mathfrak{M}p_{0}\times p_{0}\mathfrak{M}_{*})\subset(P_{0}\times p_{0}\mathfrak{M}_{*})\times(\mathfrak{M}p_{0}\times(\mathfrak{M}p_{0})^{*})\equiv T^{*}(T_{*}P_{0}).

Finally the weak symplectic form
\omega
defines a Lie Poisson structure on the algebra
\mathfrak{A}(T_{*}P_{0}))
associated to
T^{\flat}(T_{*}P_{0})
.
In particular,
{\bf P}:=(\omega_{0}^{\flat})^{-1}
is a a Poisson anchor.

On the other hand, we have the pair groupoid
T_{*}P_{0}\times T_{*}P_{0}\rightrightarrows T_{*}P_{0}
and a Poisson anchor

{\bf P}\times{\bf P}:T^{\flat}(T_{*}P_{0}\times T_{*}P_{0})\to T(T_{*}P_{0}\times T_{*}P_{0})

In
[
OJS18
]
, p. 40, it is proved:

Proposition A.10
.

The groupoid
T_{*}P_{0}\times T_{*}P_{0}\rightrightarrows T_{*}P_{0}
has a sub-Poisson groupoid structure associated to the following commutative diagram of groupoid morphisms:

(A.3)

In fact, the reader will find in
[
OJS18
]
an exact sequence of sub-Poisson groupoids.

A.7
Fredholm Groupoid Associated to the Von Neumann Algebra
\mathfrak{M}=\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})

In this section, we consider the Von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{M}=\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
and all results stated from
[
OJS18
]
, 6.3.

At first, recall that the Banach predual
(\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}))_{*}
is
\mathsf{L}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
and the pairing between
\mathsf{L}_{1}({\mathcal{H}})
and
\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
is given by:

\langle A,B\rangle:=\mathsf{Tr}(AB)

The set
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}))
of orthogonal projections is isomorphic to
\mathcal{L}({\mathcal{H}})
and the associated set
\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}))
is

\{A\in\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}),\;:\;\textrm{Im }A=\overline{\textrm{Im}A}\}

By the way, the groupoid
\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}))\rightrightarrows\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}))
can be identified with the groupoid
\mathcal{G}{\mathcal{H}})\rightrightarrows\mathcal{L}({\mathcal{H}})
where
{\bf s}(A)=(\ker A)^{\perp}
and
{\bf t}(A)=\textrm{Im}A
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{N}({\mathcal{H}})
be the set of projections of rank
N
and we set

\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}({\mathcal{H}})=\bigcup_{N=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{L}_{N}({\mathcal{H}})\textrm{ and }\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{fin}}({\mathcal{H}})={\bf s}^{-1}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}({\mathcal{H}}))\cap{\bf t}^{-1}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}({\mathcal{H}})).

Then
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{fin}}({\mathcal{H}})\rightrightarrows\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}({\mathcal{H}})))
is the groupoid of finite rank operators

On the other hand, consider the groupoid
\mathcal{G}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\rightrightarrows\mathcal{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})))
of infinitely dimensional range partially invertible operators. We have an involution

\perp:\mathcal{L}({\mathcal{H}})\to\mathcal{L}({\mathcal{H}})
defined by
\perp(p):=p^{\perp}:=1-p
for
p\in\mathcal{L}({\mathcal{H}})
.

Then
\perp
is an automorphism of
\mathcal{L}({\mathcal{H}})
and
\perp\circ\perp=Id_{\mathcal{L}({\mathcal{H}})}
.

We consider the subgroupoid
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{Fred}}({\mathcal{H}})\rightrightarrows\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Fred}}({\mathcal{H}}))))
of
\mathcal{G}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\rightrightarrows\mathcal{L}_{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})))
defined in the following way:

if
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Fred}}({\mathcal{H}}):=\perp(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{fin}}({\mathcal{H}}))
then
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{Fred}}({\mathcal{H}})={\bf s}^{-1}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Fred}}({\mathcal{H}}))\cap{\bf t}^{-1}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Fred}}({\mathcal{H}}))

Now, as previously, for any fixed
p_{0}\in\mathcal{L}({\mathcal{H}})
, we can consider
P_{0}^{\mathsf{Fred}}:={\bf s}^{-1}(p_{0})\cap\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{Fred}}({\mathcal{H}})
. To
P_{0}
is associated a weak Banach subbundle
T_{*}P_{0}^{\mathsf{Fred}}
of
T^{*}P_{0}^{\mathsf{Fred}}
whose fibers are the predual of fibers of
TP_{0}^{\mathsf{Fred}}
. Then by same arguments as in the proof of Proposition
A.10
we have:

Proposition A.11
.

The groupoid
T_{*}P_{0}^{\mathsf{Fred}}\times T_{*}P_{0}^{\mathsf{Fred}}\rightrightarrows T_{*}P^{\mathsf{Fred}}_{0}
has a sub-Poisson groupoid structure associated to the following commutative diagram of groupoid morphisms:

\textstyle{T^{\flat}(T_{*}P_{0}^{\mathsf{Fred}}\times T_{*}P_{0}^{\mathsf{Fred}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\bf P}\times{\bf P}}
\textstyle{T(T_{*}P_{0}^{\mathsf{Fred}}\times T_{*}P_{0}^{\mathsf{Fred}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T^{\flat}(T_{*}P_{0}^{\mathsf{Fred}})\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\bf P}}
\textstyle{T(T_{*}P_{0}^{\mathsf{Fred}})}

(A.4)
